- `ass:j-specialization-continuity`: pinned reading: For each fixed $k\in\{0,\dots,K-1\}$ and $j\in\{0,\dots,l_k-1\}$ to which part~(i) is applied, its continuity hypothesis is $\mu_{1,k\mid X}(\{\nu_{j,k}(X)\})=\mu_{1,k\mid X}(\{\nu_{j+1,k}(X)\})=0$ almost surely under the marginal law of $X$. (The proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}(i) fixes the band indices and uses continuity of the distribution function at the two boundaries to identify their probability levels and exclude boundary atoms.)
- `ass:ot-formulation-untreated-compatibility`: pinned reading: The untreated compatibility equality holds for $\mathbb P_{Z,X}$-almost every $(z,x)$, where $\mathbb P_{Z,X}$ is the $(Z,X)$-marginal of $\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}$. (Equation $\eqref{eq:ot_formulation}$ explicitly imposes the same compatibility constraints for $\mathbb P_{Z,X}$-almost every $(z,x)$.)
- `ass:overlap-policy`: pinned reading: For every $x\in\mathcal X$ and every nonempty $T_{j,k}$, $\tau_{j,k}(x)\geqslant c_\pi$, where $c_\pi>0$ is the same constant used for $\pi_k$ in \cref{asp:overlap}. (Both clauses belong to the single quantified overlap assumption. The proof of \cref{thm:dml_normality} uses this assumption to bound the inverse-probability weights uniformly.)
- `ass:phi-smooth-derivative`: pinned reading: There exist finite constants $C,L\geqslant0$ such that, for all $z\in\mathcal Z$, $x\in\mathcal X$, and $p,p'\in[0,1]$, $\|\partial\phi/\partial p(z,x,p)\|\leqslant C$ and $\|\partial\phi/\partial p(z,x,p)-\partial\phi/\partial p(z,x,p')\|\leqslant L\|p-p'\|$. (The exact-recovery argument for \cref{thm:dml_normality} uses a uniformly bounded derivative, and (\ref{eq:pf_dml_bias}) uses its uniformly Lipschitz derivative for the quadratic policy remainder.)
- `ass:phi-smooth-differentiable`: pinned reading: For every $z\in\mathcal Z$ and $x\in\mathcal X$, $p\mapsto\phi(z,x,p)$ is differentiable on $(0,1)$ and has finite right and left derivatives at $p=0$ and $p=1$, respectively; these are its endpoint values of $\partial\phi/\partial p$. (The policy map has domain $\mathcal Z\times\mathcal X\times[0,1]$. The estimation procedure clips $\hat p$ to this interval, and the policy Taylor remainder in (\ref{eq:pf_dml_bias}) uses differentiation within that domain.)
- `ass:structure-consistency`: pinned reading: $Y=WY(1)+(1-W)Y(0)$ almost surely under the structural population law. (Assumption \ref{asp:structure} states consistency as a structural equality of random variables; the subsequent observational derivation states conditional identities almost everywhere, consistently with the standard almost-sure convention.)
- `ass:structure-threshold-crossing`: pinned reading: $W=\indicator(U\leqslant p(Z,X))$ almost surely under the structural population law. (The threshold-crossing clause of Assumption \ref{asp:structure} is a structural equality of random variables. The derivation preceding equation \ref{eq:canonical_submeasures} uses it to establish observational identities almost everywhere.)
- `ass:structure-uniform-resistance`: pinned reading: $\mathcal L(U\mid X=x)=\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$ for $\mathbb P_X$-almost every $x$. (Assumption \ref{asp:structure} uses conditional-distribution notation without a pointwise covariate quantifier, while its common-support clause explicitly says every covariate value. The surrounding prose also recognizes that conditional probabilities are identified only almost surely.)
- `ass:target-weight-identifiable`: pinned reading: Any two structural laws satisfying Structural Assumptions and inducing the same $\mathbb P_{obs}$ give the same $\omega(x,u)$ for $\mathbb P_{obs}(\mathrm dx)\,\mathrm du$-almost every $(x,u)$. (The paragraph following Structural Assumptions states that the observed law identifies conditional probabilities almost surely; equation (\ref{eq:omega_form}) constructs weights from such conditional expectations, and equation (\ref{eq:target_mte}) integrates against the covariate marginal and Lebesgue measure.)
- `def:binary-score`: pinned reading: In this definition, $\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{\mathrm{prod}}$ and $\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}$ are the binary-outcome components of Section~\ref{app:scores_discrete}, and $\psi_{\Delta_K}$ is the common tail component of Section~\ref{app:scores_continuous}. (The binary nuisance-estimation subsection introduces this display and immediately directs the reader to Section~\ref{app:scores_discrete} for its component formulas.)
- `def:boundary-indicator-means`: pinned reading: For $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, interpret $\nu_{j,k}(x)=Q_{Y,k\mid x}(\kappa_{j,k}(x))$ using $Q_{Y,k\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,k\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$ for $0<t<1$, with $\nu_{0,k}(x)=y_{\min}$. (Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} defines the regular boundaries through the quantile function and explicitly fixes the lower boundary. The lower generalized inverse is the standard quantile-function convention; the propensity-bin and separation conventions place regular thresholds inside $(0,1)$.)
- `def:boundary-treated-law`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, use the standard lower generalized quantile $Q_{1,\underline p\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,\underline p\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$. (The Notation subsection uses quantile functions without imposing atomlessness or strict monotonicity. Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction} integrates this quantile against Lebesgue measure, so the standard generalized-inverse convention applies and endpoint values do not affect the population integrals.)
- `def:continuous-instrument`: pinned reading: Off-grid evaluations of $\widehat C(\tau\mid x)$ use linear interpolation between adjacent cumulative-integral values on $\{0,\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_{M_Q-1},1\}$, whose values are computed by the trapezoid rule. (The Target Evaluation paragraph in the Continuous Instrument estimation appendix explicitly specifies interpolation between adjacent cumulative-integral grid values for off-grid lookup.)
- `def:continuous-learners-clipped-grid`: pinned reading: The superscript $c$ in $\widetilde Q_m^c$ labels clipping to $[y_{\min},y_{\max}]$ and does not denote the transport cost $c$. (The defining minimum-maximum formula is immediately described as the clipped grid fit before rearrangement.)
- `def:continuous-learners-selected-law`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, $Q^{\mathrm{sel}}_{1,n\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu^{\mathrm{sel}}_{1,n\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$. (This is the standard lower generalized-inverse reading of the probability-measure quantile notation introduced in the paper's notation section and used for the selected-law target.)
- `def:continuous-lower-bound-quantiles`: pinned reading: For each probability law $\mu$ used here, $Q_\mu(t)=\inf\{a\in\mathbb R:\mu(( -\infty,a])\geqslant t\}$ for $t\in(0,1)$, with the displayed quantile integrals interpreted as Lebesgue integrals over $(0,1)$. (This is the standard generalized-inverse convention for the quantiles in 1D Optimal Transport with Product Cost, which General Closed-Form Sharp Bounds invokes; the settled quantile domains are $(0,1)$.)
- `def:continuous-outcome-score`: pinned reading: In this definition, all components of $\psi(O;\eta)$ are those of Section~\ref{app:scores_continuous}, in particular $\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}=\gamma_{j,k}\psi_{j-1,k}^{J}+J_{j-1,k}(\indicator(Z\in\bigcup_{l=j}^{l_k}T_{l,k})-\gamma_{j,k})$. (The sentence immediately following the display states that all components are given in Section~\ref{app:scores_continuous}; that subsection explicitly supplies the fractional product formula and its index shift.)
- `def:covariate-continuous-lower-bound-quantiles`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, use the lower generalized inverses $Q_{Y,k\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,k\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$ and $Q_{\omega,k\mid x}(t)=\inf\{v\in\mathbb R:F_{\omega,k\mid x}(v)\geqslant t\}$. (The notation section uses the standard quantile convention, and the measurability step in the proof of the Conditional General Bound explicitly constructs jointly measurable generalized inverses.)
- `def:covariate-continuous-lower-bound-rank-kernel`: pinned reading: Interpret the kernel formula with any fixed jointly measurable $\mathcal Y$-valued extension of $Q_{Y,k\mid x}$ to ranks $0$ and $1$; the asserted gap marginal and attaining objective hold for every such extension. (The sharpness step in the proof of the Conditional General Bound constructs a probability kernel and states that the randomized rank is uniform. Consequently endpoint ranks have zero probability under the product law of the independent gap and auxiliary draws.)
- `def:discrete-lower-bound-quantiles`: pinned reading: For $i=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $0<t<1$, $Q_{Y,i}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,i}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$ and $Q_{\omega,i}(t)=\inf\{v\in\mathbb R:\mathbb P(\omega(U)\leqslant v)\geqslant t\}$, where $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(p_i,p_{i+1})$. - `def:dml-binary-rearrangement`: pinned reading: At each evaluation covariate $X_i$, $(\hat P^*_{1,1}(X_i),\ldots,\hat P^*_{1,K}(X_i))$ is the nondecreasing sort of the clipped vector $(\hat P_{1,1}(X_i),\ldots,\hat P_{1,K}(X_i))$, with $\hat P^*_{1,0}(X_i)=0$; this same vector is used in every score plug-in and residual, without a separate gap projection. (The third step of the binary-outcome algorithm and the appendix's Rearrangement paragraph explicitly give this operation and its use throughout the scores.)
- `def:dml-continuous-outcome-algorithm`: pinned reading: The structural-zero assignment in Algorithm~\ref{alg:dml_continuous_outcome} is $\widehat S_0=\{z:\bar p_2(z)\leqslant\rho_n\}$, with deterministic $\rho_n\downarrow0$ satisfying $n^{1/4}\rho_n\to\infty$. (The Level Sets step in Section~\ref{app:procedure_continuous_outcome} explicitly defines this assignment and removes precisely its baseline vertices from the graph.)
- `def:dml-estimation-procedure`: pinned reading: Two remaining labeled vertices are connected exactly when their averaged values differ by less than $c_{\mathrm{gap}}/2$. (Both Algorithm~\ref{alg:dml_continuous_outcome} and the Level Sets step in Section~\ref{app:procedure_continuous_outcome} explicitly use ``less than''.)
- `def:dml-level-recovery`: pinned reading: The estimated baseline bins used to index $\widehat T_{j,k}$ include their left endpoints and exclude their right endpoints for $k<K$; the final bin for $k=K$ includes both endpoints, including $1$. (The baseline and alternative instrument level-set definition specifies $[p_k(x),p_{k+1}(x))$ for $k<K$ and $[p_K(x),1]$ for the final bin; the recovery paragraph explicitly uses those bin conventions.)
- `def:dml-normality-binary-nuisances`: pinned reading: The binary nuisance list supplements the separately fitted first-stage propensity, so $\eta(x)=\{p(z,x),p_k(x),P_{1,k}(x),\pi_k(x),\tau_{j,k}(x),a_{j,k}(z,x),\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}(x),\gamma_{j,k}(x),\gamma_K(x)\}_{j,k}$, with $p_0=P_{1,0}=0$ fixed and no fitted $\pi_0$. (Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality} separately imposes the instrument-specific first-stage rate before specifying the remaining score nuisances. Section~\ref{app:procedure_binary_outcome} explicitly prints the full vector including $p(z,x)$ and states the structural-zero conventions.)
- `def:j-specialization-boundaries`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, use the standard lower generalized quantile $Q_{Y,k\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,k\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$. (The Notation subsection calls these quantile functions, and the binary-outcome part of Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} explicitly uses an atomic law. This fixes generalized inversion rather than ordinary inversion; the displayed formula specifies the standard lower-quantile convention at flat pieces.)
- `def:mte`: pinned reading: $\text{MTE}(x,u)$ denotes a jointly measurable version of $\mathbb E[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid X=x,U=u]$, determined only for $(\mathbb P_X\otimes\mathrm du)$-almost every $(x,u)$. (The structural assumption specifies $U\mid X\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$, and the target in equation~(\ref{eq:target_mte}) integrates this conditional mean against $\omega(X,u)$.)
- `def:ot-formulation`: pinned reading: Every constraint in $\eqref{eq:ot_formulation}$ is imposed simultaneously for both $w\in\{0,1\}$. (The sentence immediately preceding equation $\eqref{eq:ot_formulation}$ explicitly says that every constraint is imposed for both treatment states.)
- `def:quantile-endpoint-convention`: pinned reading: The endpoint-invariance assertion for population Lebesgue integrals concerns changes only at $\tau\in\{0,1\}$ of a fixed population quantile; the two permitted fitted interpolants may differ on $[0,M_Q^{-1}]\cup[1-M_Q^{-1},1]$. (The endpoint-convention paragraph explicitly distinguishes a grid-integral difference of order $O(M_Q^{-1})$ from invariance of population Lebesgue integrals.)
- `def:range-decomposition-cov`: pinned reading: For $\mathbb P_X$-almost every $x$, the intervals $L_k(x)$, $1\leqslant k\leqslant K_x$, the gaps $G_k(x)$, $0\leqslant k<K_x$, and the tail $(\overline p_{K_x}(x),1]$ partition $[0,1]$ up to Lebesgue-null endpoints. (The introduction to the definition gives almost-sure covariate scope. The maximal-interval decomposition step in the proof of the Conditional General Bound explicitly states the index ranges and the partition up to Lebesgue-null endpoints.)
- `def:range-regions`: pinned reading: The partition assertion means $\int_{[0,1]\setminus\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}L_k\,\cup\,\bigcup_{k=0}^{K-1}G_k\,\cup\,(\overline p_K,1]\right)}\mathrm du=0$. (Structural Assumptions gives uniform latent resistance, and the objective decomposition immediately following the region definition uses Lebesgue integrals over precisely these regions in deriving General Closed-Form Sharp Bounds.)
- `def:wasserstein-distance`: pinned reading: For every probability measure $\mu$ on $\mathbb R$ and $0<t<1$, $Q_\mu(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu(( -\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$; the integral defining $W_1$ is a Lebesgue integral over $(0,1)$ and requires no endpoint values. (The notation paragraph calls $Q_\mu$ the quantile function, invoking the standard lower generalized-inverse convention. Its displayed formula for $W_1$ integrates quantiles against Lebesgue measure.)
- `lem:orthogonality-discrete`: pinned reading: Part~\textup{(i)} assumes the common hypotheses and continuous-outcome case of Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}; part~\textup{(ii)} assumes its common hypotheses and binary-outcome case. (Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete} explicitly references the respective theorem parts, and its separate proofs use the density conditions for continuous boundaries and the discrete-outcome gap for binary boundaries.)
- `prop:continuous-reduction`: pinned reading: For $\underline p(x)>0$ and $0<t<1$, $Q_{1,\underline p\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,\underline p\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$; at $t=0,1$ use the endpoint extension of the initial-gap outcome quantile in the referenced bound. (The boundary-treated-law definition identifies this law with the outcome law on the initial gap. The covariate continuous lower-bound theorem supplies the binding generalized-inverse reading and endpoint extension; the continuous-reduction proof uses that same gap law.)
- `prop:j-specialization`: pinned reading: The boundary quantile uses the bounded-domain lower generalized inverse $Q_{Y,k\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in\mathcal Y:\mu_{1,k\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$ for $0\leqslant t<1$, so $Q_{Y,k\mid x}(0)=y_{\min}$. (The standard lower generalized-inverse convention supplies the interior quantiles. The known compact outcome domain and the structural boundary $\nu_{0,k}=y_{\min}$ in Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} supply the bounded extension at zero; the policy-level definition explicitly permits $q_{1,k}=p_k$. The proof of part (i) uses the resulting boundary relation $F(\nu_{j,k})=\kappa_{j,k}$ under its continuity hypothesis.)
- `thm:1d-ot`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, $(Q_\mu(t),Q_\nu(t))=(\inf\{a\in\mathbb R:\mu(( -\infty,a])\geqslant t\},\inf\{b\in\mathbb R:\nu(( -\infty,b])\geqslant t\})$. (The supplied Villani source, Section 2.2.1, printed page 73, defines the generalized inverse using a strict inequality. The theorem's integrals and uniform coupling draws do not use endpoint values. The paper fixes no convention; the non-strict lower generalized inverse is used throughout (the strict inverse of the cited source differs from it at countably many levels only, which no statement uses).)
- `thm:continuous-primitive-rate`: pinned reading: For $0<t<1$, $Q^{\mathrm{sel}}_{1,n\mid x}(t)=\inf\{y\in[y_{\min},y_{\max}]:\mu^{\mathrm{sel}}_{1,n\mid x}(( -\infty,y])\geqslant t\}$. (This is the standard lower generalized-inverse convention for the selected-law quantile function in \cref{asp:continuous_learners}; the proof compares the sorted fitted grid with this monotone population quantile vector. Endpoint interpolation is separately specified in \cref{app:procedure_continuous_instrument}.)
- `thm:covariate-continuous-lower-bound`: pinned reading: For every nonempty conditional gap $G_k(x)$ and $0<t<1$, $(Q_{Y,k\mid x}(t),Q_{\omega,k\mid x}(t))=(\inf\{y\in\mathbb R:\mu_{1,k\mid x}(\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,y])\geqslant t\},\inf\{v\in\mathbb R:F_{\omega,k\mid x}(v)\geqslant t\})$. (The pinned quantile convention of \cref{thm:1d_ot}, applied to the conditional outcome and weight laws, and the generalized inverses used in the sharpness construction of \cref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} determine this reading.)
- `thm:dml-normality`: pinned reading: Every nuisance-error norm $\\|\hat f-f\\|_{L_2}$ denotes $\bigl(\mathbb E_X[\|\hat f(X)-f(X)\|^2]\bigr)^{1/2}$, integrating over the population marginal law of $X$ with the fitted function held fixed. (The exact-recovery argument in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality} bounds training-conditional population mean errors by these norms, and both remainder arguments integrate nuisance errors over an independent evaluation draw.)
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal{Y}`: $\mathcal Y$ is the fixed, known, compact outcome domain contained in $\mathbb R$, equipped with its Borel $\sigma$-field, with known feasible endpoints $y_{\min}=\min\mathcal Y$ and $y_{\max}=\max\mathcal Y$. — The opening paragraph of Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte} explicitly specifies compact Borel domains and treats the outcome domain and its extrema as known.
- reading of the symbol `Y`: $Y$ denotes the observed outcome taking values in the known compact Borel domain $\mathcal Y\subset\mathbb R$, with $y_{\min}\leqslant Y\leqslant y_{\max}$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In part~\textup{(ii)}, $Y\in\{0,1\}$. — Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte} specifies the observed outcome, compact domains, and known endpoints; Assumption~\ref{asp:structure} gives consistency. Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}\textup{(ii)} and Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}\textup{(ii)} specify binary outcomes.
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal{Z}`: $\mathcal Z$ is the compact standard Borel instrument domain equipped with its Borel $\sigma$-field and is the common conditional support $\mathrm{Supp}(Z\mid X=x)=\mathcal Z$ for all $x\in\mathcal X$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}, Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\mathcal Z$ is finite. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}, Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: $\mathcal Z$ has finitely many connected components. In Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\mathcal Z$ is connected. — The setup in Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte} fixes the domain conventions and Assumption~\ref{asp:structure}(iv) fixes common support; the finite, finite-component, and connected restrictions are stated in the corresponding settings.
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal{X}`: $\mathcal X\subset\mathbb R^{d_X}$ is the compact covariate domain, equipped with its Borel $\sigma$-field and regarded as a standard Borel space. — The setup in Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte}, at flat.tex line 159, defines $\mathcal X\subset\mathbb R^{d_X}$ and states that all domains are compact, carry their Borel $\sigma$-fields, and are standard Borel spaces.
- reading of the symbol `X`: $X$ denotes observed covariate information taking values in the compact Borel domain $\mathcal X\subset\mathbb R^{d_X}$; $x$ denotes a conditioning value, and expectations indexed by $X$ average over its marginal law. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: $X$ is almost surely constant, so conditional covariate arguments are suppressed. — Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte} specifies the covariate domain; Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} averages its conditional bound over $X$. Theorems~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound} and~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound} explicitly assume $X$ almost surely constant.
- reading of the symbol `U`: $U$ denotes the latent resistance in the threshold-crossing model, invariant under the policy change, with interval-uniform uses denoting its law restricted to the indicated resistance interval. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}: In defining $Q_{\omega,i}$, $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(p_i,p_{i+1})$ denotes the resistance draw conditional on $U\in(p_i,p_{i+1}]$, for $i=0,\dots,K-1$ with $p_i<p_{i+1}$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: In defining $Q_{\omega,k}$, $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(G_k)$ denotes the resistance draw restricted to the nonempty gap $G_k$, for $k=0,\dots,K-1$; no gap-specific law is defined for an empty gap. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: For fixed $x$ and a nonempty $G_k(x)$, the draw $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(G_k(x))$ used to define $Q_{\omega,k\mid x}$ is the resistance law conditional on $X=x$ and $U\in G_k(x)$, for $k=0,\dots,K_x-1$; in the randomized rank construction this gap draw is independent of $S\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$. — Assumption~\ref{asp:structure} specifies the normalized conditional resistance law, and line 179 specifies policy invariance; line 404 identifies the conditional interval law, while Theorems~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound},~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}, and~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} explicitly specify the restricted draws used for weight quantiles.
- reading of the symbol `p`: $p(z,x)=\mathbb P(W=1\mid Z=z,X=x)$ denotes the baseline population conditional treatment propensity, with values in $[0,1]$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}: Since $X$ is almost surely constant, $p$ is written as $p(z)$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: Since $X$ is almost surely constant, $p$ is written as $p(z)$, using its continuous version on each connected component of $\mathcal Z$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}, Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: For almost every $x$, $z\mapsto p(z,x)$ is the version continuous on each connected component of $\mathcal Z$, so its range and extrema are taken over that version throughout the conditional support. — Assumption~\ref{asp:structure}(iii) defines the propensity; the subsequent setup paragraph and Assumption~\ref{asp:continuity_p} specify its continuous version; Section~\ref{subsubsec:discrete_iv} suppresses constant covariates.
- reading of the symbol `q`: $q:\mathcal Z\times\mathcal X\to[0,1]$ denotes the alternative-policy propensity function determining $W^q=\indicator(U\leqslant q(Z,X))$ with the same latent resistance $U$ as under the baseline policy. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}, Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $q(z,x)=\phi(z,x,p(z,x))$, where the known map $\phi:\mathcal Z\times\mathcal X\times[0,1]\to[0,1]$ is jointly measurable. — Section~\ref{subsec:iv_prte} introduces the counterfactual propensity and invariant resistance; Section~\ref{sec:est_inf} specifies the known policy map.
- reading of the symbol `\phi`: $\phi:\mathcal Z\times\mathcal X\times[0,1]\to[0,1]$ is the known jointly measurable policy map specifying $q(z,x)=\phi(z,x,p(z,x))$ and the substitution $\hat q(z,x)=\phi(z,x,\hat p(z,x))$. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\phi(z,x,p)$ is differentiable in $p$, and $\partial\phi/\partial p$ is bounded and uniformly Lipschitz in $p$ over $z\in\mathcal Z$ and $x\in\mathcal X$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\|\phi(z,x,p')-\phi(z,x,p)\|\leqslant L_\phi\|p'-p\|$ for all $(z,x,p,p')\in\mathcal Z\times\mathcal X\times[0,1]^2$, with $L_\phi<\infty$. — Flat.tex line 726 defines the map. Assumption~\ref{asp:phi_smooth} at line 750 supplies the inference regularity, while Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate} at lines 3010--3013 supplies uniform Lipschitz regularity. Lines 834 and 1761 apply the original map to fitted propensities.
- reading of the symbol `P`: $P=p(Z,X)$ is the observed baseline propensity index obtained by evaluating the population propensity function at $(Z,X)$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: The conditional propensity support is $\operatorname{supp}(P\mid X=x)=[\underline p(x),\overline p(x)]$ in the connected-instrument setting. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: With $X$ almost surely constant, the covariate argument is suppressed and $P=p(Z)$. — Line 284 introduces $P=p(Z,X)$; lines 876--883 repeat the definition and specify its connected conditional support. The finite-instrument results use the finitely many propensity values induced by $\mathcal Z$.
- reading of the symbol `W^q`: $W^q=\indicator(U\leqslant q(Z,X))$ uses the same latent resistance $U$ as baseline treatment $W$; $U$ is invariant to the policy change. — The defining policy paragraph at flat.tex line 179 explicitly assumes that the latent variable $U$ is invariant to the policy change.
- reading of the symbol `\omega`: $\omega:\mathcal X\times[0,1]\to\mathbb R$ is the population target weight, jointly measurable and identifiable from the observed law, with $\mathbb E_X[\int_0^1\|\omega(X,u)\|\,\mathrm du]<\infty$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: With $X$ almost surely constant, $\omega$ is written as a function $\omega(u)$ of latent resistance alone. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}, Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\omega(x,u)=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}[\indicator(u\leqslant q(Z,X))-\indicator(u\leqslant p(Z,X))\mid X=x]$, where $q(z,x)=\phi(z,x,p(z,x))$ and $\phi$ is known; the target is the unscaled numerator $\mathbb E[Y^q-Y]$. — Assumption~\ref{asp:target_weight} gives the general convention; Section~\ref{subsec:cf_no_cov} suppresses covariates; Section~\ref{sec:est_inf} explicitly adopts the unscaled policy-numerator weight.
- reading of the symbol `\theta_\omega`: $\theta_\omega=\mathbb E_X\int_0^1\text{MTE}(X,u)\omega(X,u)\,\mathrm du$ denotes the target under the latent law being considered, equivalently $\mathbb E[Y(1)\omega(X,U)]-\mathbb E[Y(0)\omega(X,U)]$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness}: When written with candidate-law arguments, $\theta_\omega(\pi_0,\pi_1)=\mathbb E_{\pi_1}[Y(1)\omega(X,U)]-\mathbb E_{\pi_0}[Y(0)\omega(X,U)]$, with $(\pi_0,\pi_1)\in\Gamma(\mathbb P_{obs})$; its value varies with the candidate marginal laws. — Equation (\ref{eq:target_mte}) defines the target; the display at flat.tex line 269 defines its evaluation at marginal laws, used in (\ref{eq:ot_formulation}) and the discussion following Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness}.
- reading of the symbol `\theta_{\omega,w}`: For $w\in\{0,1\}$, $\theta_{\omega,w}(\pi_w)=\mathbb E_{\pi_w}[Y(w)\omega(X,U)]$ is the component functional of the candidate joint law $\pi_w$ of $(Y(w),U,X)$, so that $\theta_\omega=\theta_{\omega,1}-\theta_{\omega,0}$. — The target decomposition before (\ref{eq:ot_formulation}) and the component-interval paragraph following (\ref{eq:ot_formulation_1}) specify the law argument and the two component indices.
- reading of the symbol `\hat{\underline{\theta}}_{\omega,1}`: $\hat{\underline\theta}_{\omega,1}$ denotes the sample-based estimator of the treated-component sharp lower bound, formed by averaging fitted score contributions over $I_2$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\hat{\underline\theta}_{\omega,1}=\|I_2\|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_2}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left(\hat\psi_{\text{full},k}^{\text{prod}}(O_i;\hat\eta)+\sum_{j=1}^{l_k}\hat\psi_{j,k}^{\text{prod}}(O_i;\hat\eta)\right)+\hat\psi_{\Delta_K}(O_i;\hat\eta)\right]$, with the nuisance list and score formulas appropriate to the continuous or binary outcome regime. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\hat{\underline\theta}_{\omega,1}=\|I_2\|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_2}(\hat\psi_1(O_i)+\hat\psi_2(O_i)+\hat\psi_3(O_i))$, where the three contributions substitute estimated nuisances into the three terms of equation~\ref{eq:continuous_simplified_target}, with predictions clipped to their known supports. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: The nuisance estimates are those supplied by Algorithm~\ref{alg:continuous_instrument}, including the local-linear regression and treated boundary conditional quantile construction. — Equations~\ref{eq:estimator_dml} and~\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument} give distinct score averages; the paragraph following Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate} specifies the concrete construction used for Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}.
- reading of the symbol `\pi_w`: For $w\in\{0,1\}$, $\pi_w$ denotes a joint probability law of $(Y(w),U,X)$, including a candidate such law when varied in the CCOT program, and $\pi_w(\cdot\mid u,x)$ denotes a measurable regular conditional outcome law determined only $\pi_w$-almost everywhere with respect to its $(U,X)$-marginal. — Line 268 defines the joint law and its conditional outcome distribution; equation~(\ref{eq:ot_formulation}) varies these laws; the disintegration paragraph in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness}, following (C1)--(C3), specifies the almost-everywhere conditional kernels.
- reading of the symbol `\tilde{\pi}`: $\tilde{\pi}$ is a probability measure on $\mathcal Y^2\times[0,1]\times\mathcal X$, interpreted as a candidate joint law of $(Y(0),Y(1),U,X)$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness}: Its marginals satisfy $(\tilde{\pi}_0,\tilde{\pi}_1)\in\Gamma(\mathbb P_{obs})$, hence its $(U,X)$-marginal is $\mathrm du\,\mathbb P_{obs}(\mathrm dx)$; the coupling of $Y(0)$ and $Y(1)$ conditional on $(U,X)$ is unrestricted apart from those marginal constraints. — Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness} quantifies over any such measure with feasible marginals; its proof explicitly preserves its conditional coupling and calls independent coupling only one feasible choice.
- reading of the symbol `\tilde{\pi}_w`: For $w\in\{0,1\}$, $\tilde{\pi}_w$ is the $(Y(w),U,X)$-marginal of $\tilde{\pi}$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness}: The pair satisfies $(\tilde{\pi}_0,\tilde{\pi}_1)\in\Gamma(\mathbb P_{obs})$, so $\tilde{\pi}_w(\mathrm du,\mathrm dx)=\mathrm du\,\mathbb P_{obs}(\mathrm dx)$ and its conditional outcome kernels satisfy the observational constraints in (\ref{eq:ot_formulation}); these kernels are defined up to $(\mathrm du\otimes\mathbb P_{obs}(\mathrm dx))$-null sets. — Proposition~\ref{prop:sharpness} defines the marginals and requires their pair to belong to $\Gamma(\mathbb P_{obs})$; its proof lists (C1)--(C3) and disintegrates the measures.
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal{R}_w`: $\mathcal R_w$ is the selected-region map for $w\in\{0,1\}$ and $p\in[0,1]$, with $\mathcal R_1(p)=(0,p]$ and $\mathcal R_0(p)=(p,1]$, including empty intervals at the corresponding boundary propensities. — The disintegration paragraph preceding equation~\ref{eq:canonical_submeasures} explicitly defines both selected-region maps.
- reading of the symbol `H_{w,x}`: For $w\in\{0,1\}$ and almost every $x$, $H_{w,x}$ is the unique total-variation-continuous version on $\operatorname{supp}(P\mid X=x)$ of $\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(Y\in\mathrm dy,W=w\mid P=p,X=x)$, including support endpoints; its total mass is $p$ for $w=1$ and $1-p$ for $w=0$, and without covariates it is written $H_w$. — The canonical-version construction at flat.tex lines 282--295 extends the almost-sure observed conditional submeasures continuously and uniquely to the conditional propensity support using the integrals over $(0,p]$ and $(p,1]$.
- reading of the symbol `H_w`: $H_w$, $w\in\{0,1\}$, denotes the no-covariate canonical continuous version of $\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(Y\in\mathrm{d}y,W=w\mid P=p)$ on $\operatorname{supp}(P)$, uniquely determined there by its almost-sure observed values. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: $H_1(0,\cdot)=0$ is specified even when $0$ lies outside propensity support, so the initial gap uses a structural boundary submeasure. — The canonical-version construction preceding equation~(\ref{eq:ot_formulation}) specifies uniqueness and identification on propensity support and says, verbatim, 'With no covariates we write $H_w$.' The paragraph following equation~(\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure}) specifies the structural zero boundary.
- reading of the symbol `K_{w,x}`: $K_{w,x}(\mathrm dy\mid u)$, for $w\in\{0,1\}$, is a regular conditional probability kernel for $Y(w)$ given $(U,X)=(u,x)$ under a compatible latent law, determined only $\mathbb P_{U,X}$-almost everywhere and not necessarily identified, with $H_{w,x}(p,\mathrm dy)=\int_{\mathcal R_w(p)}K_{w,x}(\mathrm dy\mid u)\,\mathrm du$ on the conditional propensity support for $\mathbb P_X$-almost every $x$. — The canonical-version construction at lines 290--295 defines the conditional kernel, obtains its cumulative integral representation, and explicitly states that the latent kernels need not be identified.
- reading of the symbol `\mu`: $\mu$ denotes a probability measure on the state space appropriate to the coupling or quantile expression in which it occurs. In Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot}: $\mu\in\mathcal P(\mathbb R)$, and either $\mu$ has bounded support and $\nu$ has a finite first moment, or vice versa. — The primer defines $\mu$ on $\mathcal A$; Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot} specializes it to $\mathbb R$, and the notation paragraph defines its real-valued quantile and Wasserstein notation.
- reading of the symbol `\nu`: $\nu$ denotes a probability measure in transport notation, a locally bound candidate real-valued function of $X$ inside the conditional quantile-regression minimization, or an unperturbed scalar quantile boundary in a local boundary-perturbation calculation. In Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot}: $\nu\in\mathcal P(\mathbb R)$, and either $\mu$ has bounded support and $\nu$ has a finite first moment, or vice versa. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In the continuous-outcome quantile-regression minimization, $\nu$ is a candidate function $\mathcal X\to\mathbb R$; the resulting population threshold is $\nu_{j,k}(x)=Q_{Y,k\mid x}(\kappa_{j,k}(x))$ for $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, while $\nu_{0,k}(x)=y_{\min}$ is fixed. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}: In the continuous-outcome proof's indicator-boundary calculation, unindexed $\nu$ denotes the scalar value of the threshold being perturbed at a given covariate value: $a_t=\nu+t\,\delta\nu$, and $\int_{\nu}^{a_t}(a_t-y)\,\mathrm{d}F(y)$ uses the oriented-integral convention when $a_t<\nu$. — The primer defines the marginal measure, and the minimization in Equation~\ref{eq:nu_estimation} is displayed at flat.tex line 1804 with the candidate evaluated as $\nu(X)$. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, at flat.tex lines 2824--2833, uses $a_t=\nu+t\,\delta\nu$ and the oriented integral from $\nu$ to $a_t$ for a generic relevant conditional law $F$.
- reading of the symbol `c`: In transport notation, $c$ is the specified cost function on the two transported state spaces; a conditional cost $c(y,u,x)$ is read at fixed $x$ as the function $(y,u)\mapsto y\omega(x,u)$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot}: $\mathcal A=\mathcal B=\mathbb R$ and $c(a,b)=a\cdot b$. — Equation~(\ref{eq:kantorovich}) introduces the generic cost, Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot} specifies the product cost, and the paragraph following equation~(\ref{eq:ot_formulation}) specifies the conditional effective cost.
- reading of the symbol `\xi`: In transport notation, $\xi\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$ is the auxiliary variable shared by both coordinates of $(Q_\mu(\xi),Q_\nu(1-\xi))$ or $(Q_\mu(\xi),Q_\nu(\xi))$. — The final sentence of Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot} specifies the law and shared-variable construction. The simulation paragraph later uses $\xi(X)$ for a separate covariate index.
- reading of the symbol `K`: $K$ is a population count in propensity decompositions and a chosen function when written as the local-linear smoothing kernel $K(t)$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}: $K=\|\{p(z):z\in\mathcal Z,\ p(z)>0\}\|$, excluding the structural boundary $p_0=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: $K$ is the number of ordered maximal closed intervals in $p(\mathcal Z)$ after merging overlaps, including degenerate singleton intervals and any interval containing zero. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $K$ counts the distinct positive baseline propensity levels at each $x$ and does not depend on $x$; $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ indexes the identified intervals and $k=K$ indexes the residual tail. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $K(t)$ is the base one-dimensional smoothing kernel in $K_{h_n}(t)=K(t/h_n)/h_n$, rather than a propensity-level count. — Section~\ref{subsubsec:discrete_iv} defines the positive-level count; equation~\ref{eq:range_decomposition} defines the maximal-interval count; Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}(i) fixes the finite estimation count across covariates; Section~\ref{app:procedure_continuous_instrument} defines $K_{h_n}(t)=K(t/h_n)/h_n$.
- reading of the symbol `p_i`: $p_i$ denotes an ordered unique positive baseline propensity level for $1\leqslant i\leqslant K$, with structural integration boundaries $p_0=0$ and $p_{K+1}=1$; a structural boundary need not be an attained positive propensity level. In Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound}: The levels satisfy $0=p_0<p_1<\cdots<p_K\leqslant p_{K+1}=1$ and are the unique positive values of $p(z)$; $p_K=1$ makes the residual interval empty. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: The levels are functions $p_i(x)$ of the covariates, with $p_0(x)=0$ and $p_{K+1}(x)=1$. The positive level count, equality classes $S_i$, and their strict ordering are invariant in $x$ under the maintained separation condition, although the numerical levels may vary with $x$; $p_i$ without a hat denotes the population level. — The finite-level construction preceding equation~(\ref{eq:ot_y_constraint}), the notation table, the conditional level sets in Section~\ref{sec:est_inf}, and Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap} specify the levels and conventions.
- reading of the symbol `I_i`: $I_i=(p_i,p_{i+1}]$ for $i=0,\ldots,K-1$, and $I_K=(p_K,1]$; the first $K$ intervals are nonempty and the residual interval is empty when $p_K=1$. — Section~\ref{subsubsec:discrete_iv} explicitly defines the intervals, their index range, and the empty-tail convention.
- reading of the symbol `\mu_{1,i}`: $\mu_{1,i}$ is the identified conditional probability law of $Y(1)$ given $U\in I_i$, defined for $i=0,\ldots,K-1$. — Equation~\ref{eq:y_identified_measure} supplies the normalized submeasure formulas; the following derivation identifies them with the conditional laws on the latent intervals.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{Y,i}`: $Q_{Y,i}:(0,1)\to\mathcal Y$ is the quantile function of the identified conditional law $\mu_{1,i}$ of $Y(1)$ given $U\in(p_i,p_{i+1}]$, for $i=0,\ldots,K-1$. — Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound} defines these quantiles only for $i=0,\ldots,K-1$; the interpretation of $\mu_{1,i}$ follows equation~\ref{eq:y_identified_measure}.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{\omega,i}`: $Q_{\omega,i}$ is the quantile function of the law of $\omega(U)$ under $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(p_i,p_{i+1})$, for $t\in(0,1)$ and $i=0,\ldots,K-1$, using the nonempty identified baseline intervals. — Theorem~\ref{thm:discrete_lower_bound} defines this quantile, and the finite-propensity-level construction specifies the nonempty identified intervals.
- reading of the symbol `\underline{p}_k`: $\underline p_k$ denotes the left endpoint of the $k$-th maximal closed interval in the propensity range ordered from left to right, allowing singleton intervals. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: The endpoints are indexed by $k=1,\ldots,K$ in $p(\mathcal Z)=\bigcup_{k=1}^K[\underline p_k,\overline p_k]$, with the additional boundary convention $\underline p_{K+1}=1$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: For almost every $x$, the endpoint is $\underline p_k(x)$, indexed by $k=1,\ldots,K_x$ in the conditional range $p(\mathcal Z,x)$, with $\underline p_{K_x+1}(x)=1$; both endpoints and the component count may vary with $x$. — The ordered decompositions (\ref{eq:range_decomposition}) and (\ref{eq:range_decomposition_cov}) explicitly specify the indices, degeneracies, and boundary conventions.
- reading of the symbol `\overline{p}_k`: $\overline p_k$ denotes the right endpoint of the $k$-th maximal closed interval in the propensity range ordered from left to right, allowing singleton intervals and using zero as the auxiliary initial endpoint. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: The endpoints are indexed by $k=1,\ldots,K$ in $p(\mathcal Z)=\bigcup_{k=1}^K[\underline p_k,\overline p_k]$, with $\overline p_0=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: For almost every $x$, the endpoint is $\overline p_k(x)$, indexed by $k=1,\ldots,K_x$ in $p(\mathcal Z,x)$, with $\overline p_0(x)=0$; endpoints and the component count may vary with $x$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}: Each conditional range component is a singleton: $\underline p_k(x)=\overline p_k(x)=p_k(x)$ for $k=1,\ldots,K$, with the structural initial endpoint $p_0(x)=\overline p_0(x)=0$. — The decompositions (\ref{eq:range_decomposition}) and (\ref{eq:range_decomposition_cov}), followed by the finite-instrument specialization preceding Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}.
- reading of the symbol `K_x`: For almost every $x$, $K_x$ is the finite number of closed maximal intervals, including singleton intervals, obtained by merging overlapping component images in $p(\mathcal Z,x)$; its value may depend on $x$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}: The instrument domain is connected, so $K_x=1$ and the sole conditional range interval is $[\underline p(x),\overline p(x)]$, possibly a singleton. — Assumption~\ref{asp:continuity_p}, equation~(\ref{eq:range_decomposition_cov}), and the maximal-interval construction in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} specify the count.
- reading of the symbol `L_k`: $L_k$ denotes the $k$-th closed maximal interval of the propensity range, ordered from left to right, including singleton intervals. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: $L_k=[\underline p_k,\overline p_k]$ for $k=1,\ldots,K$; singleton intervals contribute a Lebesgue-null integral. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: For almost every $x$, $L_k(x)=[\underline p_k(x),\overline p_k(x)]$ for $k=1,\ldots,K_x$; the intervals are those of $p(\mathcal Z,x)$ and may be singletons. — Equation~(\ref{eq:range_decomposition}) and its following paragraph define $L_k$; equation~(\ref{eq:range_decomposition_cov}) defines the covariate-indexed counterpart.
- reading of the symbol `G_k`: $G_k$ denotes the open interval between consecutive ordered propensity-range components, with the initial gap starting at zero and with the final unconstrained tail excluded from the gap indices. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: $G_k=(\overline p_k,\underline p_{k+1})$ for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, with $\overline p_0=0$; empty gaps are omitted from gap-specific definitions and contribute zero. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: $G_k(x)=(\overline p_k(x),\underline p_{k+1}(x))$ for $k=0,\ldots,K_x-1$ and almost every $x$, with $\overline p_0(x)=0$; empty conditional gaps contribute zero. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}: The propensity-range components are singletons, so $G_k(x)=(p_k(x),p_{k+1}(x))$ for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, with $p_0(x)=0$; the interval beyond $p_K(x)$ is the separate trivial tail. — The region definitions following (\ref{eq:range_decomposition}) and (\ref{eq:range_decomposition_cov}), and the empty-gap conventions following (\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure}) and (\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure_cov}).
- reading of the symbol `\mu_{1,k}`: $\mu_{1,k}$ is the identified marginal law $\mathcal L(Y(1)\mid U\in G_k)$ on each nonempty gap $G_k$, $k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$, obtained from the canonical treated submeasures, including the structural zero submeasure at the initial boundary. — Equation~(\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure}), its following boundary convention, and the gap decomposition following equation~(\ref{eq:liv_identity}) identify this marginal law.
- reading of the symbol `\mu_{1,k\mid x}`: $\mu_{1,k\mid x}$ is the population probability law of $Y(1)$ conditional on $X=x$ and $U\in G_k(x)$, defined for nonempty gaps with $k=0,\ldots,K_x-1$; empty-gap terms are omitted and contribute zero. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}: With finite $\mathcal Z$, the conditional propensity-range intervals are singletons, $G_k(x)=(p_k(x),p_{k+1}(x))$ for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, and $\mu_{1,k\mid x}$ is the corresponding conditional complier outcome law. — Equation~(\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure_cov}) defines the measure and its nonempty-gap domain; the gap argument in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} identifies it as the conditional marginal law of $Y(1)$.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{Y,k}`: $Q_{Y,k}$ is the quantile function of $\mu_{1,k}$ for $t\in(0,1)$ and $k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ such that $\overline p_k<\underline p_{k+1}$; an empty gap has zero contribution and requires no quantile function. — Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound} and equation~(\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure}) define the quantile and its nonempty-gap domain.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{\omega,k}`: $Q_{\omega,k}$ is the quantile function of the law of $\omega(U)$ under $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(G_k)$, defined for $t\in(0,1)$ and $k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ such that $\overline p_k<\underline p_{k+1}$; an empty gap has zero contribution and requires no quantile function. — Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound} defines the uniform gap law and explicitly assigns zero contribution to empty gaps; equation~(\ref{eq:range_decomposition}) and its following paragraph specify the gap indices.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{Y,k\mid x}`: $Q_{Y,k\mid x}$ is the population quantile function of $\mu_{1,k\mid x}$ for each nonempty conditional gap $G_k(x)$; it is required only on such gaps, and contributions from empty gaps are defined as zero. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}: In the finite-instrument setting, the gap is the baseline interval between $p_k(x)$ and $p_{k+1}(x)$, with $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, and $\mu_{1,k\mid x}$ is its identified conditional complier outcome law. — Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} explicitly restricts the definition to nonempty conditional gaps and sets empty-gap terms to zero. Flat.tex lines 765--778 specialize the gap law to the finite-instrument complier intervals.
- reading of the symbol `Q_{\omega,k\mid x}`: $Q_{\omega,k\mid x}$ is defined for each nonempty conditional gap $G_k(x)$, $0\leqslant k<K_x$, as the quantile function of the pushforward of $\mathrm{Unif}(G_k(x))$ by $u\mapsto\omega(x,u)$; empty-gap contributions are zero without requiring a quantile function there. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}: The conditional propensity-range intervals are singletons, so the relevant gap law is $\mathrm{Unif}((p_k(x),p_{k+1}(x)))$ for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, with $p_0(x)=0$. — Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound} and the pushforward construction at flat.tex lines 1710--1716; the finite-instrument specialization preceding Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}.
- reading of the symbol `g_1^{\mathrm c}`: $g_1^{\mathrm c}(p,x)=\int_{\mathcal Y}y\,H_{1,x}(p,\mathrm dy)$ uses the canonical population observed submeasure on the conditional propensity support, including its endpoints; it is absolutely continuous on each non-degenerate LIV interval, and its derivatives mean intervalwise Lebesgue-a.e. derivatives. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_lower_bound}: With no covariates, $g_1^{\mathrm c}(p)=\int_{\mathcal Y}y\,H_1(p,\mathrm dy)$, and derivatives are taken on the non-degenerate intervals $L_k$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous_reduction}, Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}: $g_1=g_1^{\mathrm c}$ on the connected conditional support $[\underline p(x),\overline p(x)]$. — The canonical-version discussion following Equation~(\ref{eq:canonical_submeasures}) fixes the observed version and its support; the paragraph following Equation~(\ref{eq:gap_identified_measure_cov}) explicitly fixes absolute continuity and derivative conventions.
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal A`: $\mathcal A$ is reused contextually for the first marginal's state space in the OT primer and for the explicitly specified countable measure-determining class in the LIV argument. In Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot}: $\mathcal A=\mathbb R$ is the state space carrying the first marginal $\mu$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:covariate_continuous_lower_bound}: In the LIV proof, $\mathcal A=\{\mathcal Y\cap(-\infty,r]:r\in\mathbb Q\}\cup\{\mathcal Y\}$ is the countable measure-determining class of outcome events used to obtain a common exceptional null set. — The OT primer at flat.tex line 233, Theorem~\ref{thm:1d_ot}, and the explicit class definitions at flat.tex lines 644 and 1669.
- reading of the symbol `p_k`: $p_k(x)$ denotes an ordered population baseline propensity level, with $p_k(x)=p(z,x)$ for $z\in S_k$ and $k=1,\ldots,K$, while $p_0(x)=0$ and $p_{K+1}(x)=1$ are structural integration boundaries; $K$ and the level sets $S_k$ are invariant in $x$. — The ordered positive baseline levels are introduced in the finite-instrument identification setup; the discrete estimation subsection defines $S_k$, uses $p_{K+1}=1$, and Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}\textup{(i)} makes their equality classes and ordering invariant in $x$.
- reading of the symbol `q_{j,k}`: $q_{j,k}(x)$ denotes a population policy level for $k=0,\ldots,K$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, ordered distinctly within its baseline bin, with $T_{j,k}=\{z\in\mathcal Z:q(z,x)=q_{j,k}(x)\}$; $K,l_k,T_{j,k}$ are invariant in $x$, and $q_{0,k},q_{l_k+1,k}$ are integration boundaries with empty associated instrument sets. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: For every nonempty $T_{j,k}$ in a nonfinal bin $k<K$, $q_{j,k}(x)\neq p_k(x)$; the structural boundary $q_{0,k}=p_k$ is therefore distinct from every regular policy boundary used by the displayed scores. — The policy-level definition and boundary conventions precede Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}; part~\textup{(i)} fixes the equality classes and ordering, while part~\textup{(ii)} imposes the additional boundary exclusion for inference.
- reading of the symbol `l_k`: $l_k\in\mathbb N_0$, for $k=0,\ldots,K$, counts distinct population policy levels rather than instruments, is invariant in $x$ under the stipulated invariant ordering, and excludes structural integration boundaries while retaining an actual policy value equal to one in the final bin. — The policy-level definitions preceding Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap} specify the half-open and final closed bins, ties, and structural boundaries; part (i) of that assumption makes $l_k$ invariant in $x$.
- reading of the symbol `S_k`: $S_k=\{z\in\mathcal Z:p(z,x)=p_k(x)\}$ denotes the population baseline instrument level set for $k=1,\ldots,K$, invariant in $x$ under the maintained equality-class convention; $S_0=\{z\in\mathcal Z:p(z,x)=0\}$, when nonempty, is the structural-zero class, and $\widehat S_k$ denotes a recovered set rather than $S_k$ itself. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: At $k=0$, minus-side conditional expectations and residuals are omitted and assigned zero only for notation; no treated conditional regression on $S_0$ or division by $\pi_0(X)$ is used. — The discrete estimation subsection defines the level sets, and Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}\textup{(i)} fixes their equality classes and ordering across $x$. The nuisance-estimation procedure recovers the zero class separately. Section~\ref{app:scores_continuous} omits all minus-side objects at $k=0$ and states that the score never divides by $\pi_0(X)$.
- reading of the symbol `T_{j,k}`: $T_{j,k}$ denotes the population alternative-policy equality class for $k=0,\ldots,K$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, invariant in $x$ under the stipulated invariant ordering; structural integration-boundary sets are empty, an actual policy value equal to $p_k(x)$ belongs to $T_{1,k}$, and an actual value equal to one is retained in $T_{l_K,K}$. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: For $k=0,\ldots,K-1$, every nonempty $T_{j,k}$ has $q_{j,k}(x)\neq p_k(x)$ for all $x\in\mathcal X$. — Flat.tex lines 737--745 define the equality classes and boundary exceptions; Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}(i) and the following paragraph specify invariance in $x$.
- reading of the symbol `R_x`: $R_x=\{p(z,x),q(z,x):z\in\mathcal Z\}$ is the set of distinct baseline and alternative-policy propensity values at the covariate value $x$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: Distinct elements of $R_x$ are separated by more than $c_{\text{gap}}$, uniformly in $x$; the equality classes and their strict ordering are invariant in $x$, although the numerical propensity values may vary with $x$. — Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}(i) defines $R_x$ and separately requires invariant equality classes and strict ordering.
- reading of the symbol `c_{\text{gap}}`: $c_{\text{gap}}$ is one common positive separation constant for distinct values in the combined baseline and alternative-policy range, uniformly over $x\in\mathcal X$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: Level recovery joins averaged fitted values lying within $c_{\mathrm{gap}}/2$ of each other, where $c_{\mathrm{gap}}$ denotes this same separation constant. — Assumption~\ref{asp:prop_gap}(i) gives the strict inequality and the combined range; the estimation procedure uses half this constant for graph recovery.
- reading of the symbol `c_\pi`: $c_\pi$ is a common positive overlap constant for the specified nonempty level sets, with no overlap requirement on the structural index $k=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: The same $c_\pi$ determines the clipping interval $[c_\pi/2,1]$ for $\hat\pi_k$ and $\hat\tau_{j,k}$. — Assumption~\ref{asp:overlap} and its following structural-zero convention; Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality} specifies the further clipping interval.
- reading of the symbol `c_h`: $c_h$ is one positive constant uniform over the indicated indices and almost every covariate value, including the fixed lower boundary $q_{0,k}=p_k$. — Assumption~\ref{asp:discrete_outcome_gap} and the paragraph immediately following it explicitly include $j=0$ and identify its structural-boundary meaning.
- reading of the symbol `\gamma_{\text{full},k}`: $\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}(x)$ is the population difference of the two conditional probabilities in its defining display, taking values in $[-1,1]$ for $k=0,\ldots,K$, with $\gamma_{\mathrm{full},K}\equiv0$ by empty unions; the full-interval products use $k=0,\ldots,K-1$. — Flat.tex lines 767--771 define the difference and explicitly state its signed range; Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction} gives the product indices.
- reading of the symbol `\gamma_{j,k}`: $\gamma_{j,k}(x)$ is the population conditional probability of $\bigcup_{l=j}^{l_k}T_{l,k}$, taking values in $[0,1]$ for $k=0,\ldots,K$ and $j=0,\ldots,l_k$, with $T_{0,k}=\emptyset$; the sub-interval products use $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$. — The nuisance definitions at flat.tex lines 767--771 specify the general indices; the representation in Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction} specifies the product indices.
- reading of the symbol `\gamma_K`: $\gamma_K(x)$ is the population conditional probability of $\bigcup_{j=1}^{l_K}T_{j,K}$ in the final closed policy bin $[p_K(x),1]$, taking values in $[0,1]$ and equaling zero when $l_K=0$; actual policy levels equal to one are included. — Flat.tex line 783 defines the tail probability, lines 737--745 specify the final closed bin, and lines 1792--1796 distinguish its fitted estimate.
- reading of the symbol `\pi_k`: $\pi_k(x)=\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z\in S_k\mid X=x)$ is a population conditional probability taking values in $[0,1]$, with fitted baseline nuisances indexed by $k=1,\ldots,K$; no $\pi_0$ regression or inverse-probability correction is fitted. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\pi_k(x)\geqslant c_\pi>0$ for every $x\in\mathcal X$ and $k=1,\ldots,K$. — The definition at flat.tex line 836, the structural-zero convention following Assumption~\ref{asp:overlap}, and the nuisance procedure at lines 1781--1787 specify these indices and omissions.
- reading of the symbol `\tau_{j,k}`: $\tau_{j,k}(x)=\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z\in T_{j,k}\mid X=x)$ is a population conditional probability in $[0,1]$, with fitted policy-set nuisances indexed by nonempty $T_{j,k}$, $k=0,\ldots,K$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$; structural boundary sets are empty and have probability zero. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\tau_{j,k}(x)\geqslant c_\pi>0$ for every $x\in\mathcal X$ and every nonempty $T_{j,k}$. — The policy-set boundary conventions at flat.tex lines 737--745, the definition at line 836, and the fitting procedure at line 1787 specify the sets and indices.
- reading of the symbol `a_{j,k}`: $a_{j,k}(z,x)=\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z=z\mid Z\in T_{j,k},X=x)$ is the population assignment-share function for each nonempty policy set, indexed by $k=0,\ldots,K$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, with $a_{j,k}(z,x)=0$ outside $T_{j,k}$ and $\sum_{z\in T_{j,k}}a_{j,k}(z,x)=1$ wherever the conditioning probability is positive. — Flat.tex line 783 defines the two-argument conditional share; lines 1787--1791 give its simplex-valued estimation and pooled-policy identity.
- reading of the symbol `\kappa_{j,k}`: $\kappa_{j,k}(x)$ is the population relative threshold for a nonempty baseline interval, indexed by $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=0,\ldots,l_k+1$, with $\kappa_{0,k}(x)=0$ and $\kappa_{l_k+1,k}(x)=1$. — Flat.tex lines 740--750 define the ordered policy levels and the integration-boundary conventions; lines 772--776 define their relative thresholds. Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} uses only $j=0,\ldots,l_k$ as outcome-boundary indices.
- reading of the symbol `J_{\text{full},k}`: $J_{\text{full},k}(x)$ is the population full-interval contribution $(p_{k+1}(x)-p_k(x))\mathbb E_{\mu_{1,k\mid x}}[Y]$ for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In the binary-outcome case, $J_{\text{full},k}(x)=P_{1,k+1}(x)-P_{1,k}(x)$, where $P_{1,k}(x)=\mathbb E_{\text{obs}}[YW\mid Z\in S_k,X=x]$ for $k\geqslant1$ and $P_{1,0}(x)=0$. — The nuisance-component display immediately before Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction} defines the quantity; Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}\textup{(ii)} gives its binary specialization and zero boundary convention.
- reading of the symbol `J_{j,k}`: $J_{j,k}(x)=(p_{k+1}(x)-p_k(x))\int_{\kappa_{j,k}(x)}^{\kappa_{j+1,k}(x)}Q_{Y,k\mid x}(u)\,\mathrm du$ is the population lower-bound subinterval contribution on a nondegenerate identified bin, with structural thresholds $\kappa_{0,k}=0$ and $\kappa_{l_k+1,k}=1$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction}, Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}, Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: The bound uses $J_{j-1,k}$ for $0\leq k<K$ and $1\leq j\leq l_k$. In the continuous-outcome representation, for $0\leq j<l_k$ and under the stated continuity conditions, $J_{j,k}=(p_{k+1}-p_k)\mathbb E_{\mu_{1,k\mid X}}[Y\indicator(\nu_{j,k}<Y\leqslant\nu_{j+1,k})]$; in the binary-outcome case, $J_{j-1,k}=h_{j,k}^{-}$. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In the continuous-outcome score construction the plug-in component is $J_{j,k}=p_{k+1}J_{j,k}^{+}-p_kJ_{j,k}^{-}$; at $k=0$, the minus-side conditional expectation is omitted and set to zero for notation. — The nuisance-components definition gives the quantile integral; Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} gives its continuous and binary specializations, and Section~\ref{subsubsec:continuous_outcome} gives the treated-band representation.
- reading of the symbol `\Delta_K`: $\Delta_K(x)=y_{\min}\mathbb E_{\text{obs}}[\max\{0,q(Z,X)-p_K(X)\}\mid X=x]$ is the conditional lower-bound contribution on $(p_K(x),1]$, with the final policy bin $[p_K(x),1]$ retaining instruments whose alternative propensity equals one. — The policy-level conventions immediately preceding Assumption~\ref{asp:phi_smooth} specify the closed final bin and retention of the value one; the nuisance-component display preceding Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete_reduction} defines $\Delta_K$ and derives it from the nonnegative tail weight by Fubini.
- reading of the symbol `\nu_{j,k}`: $\nu_{j,k}$ denotes the population boundary function for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=0,\ldots,l_k$, with $\nu_{0,k}(x)\equiv y_{\min}$ and $\nu_{j,k}(x)=Q_{Y,k\mid x}(\kappa_{j,k}(x))$ for $j\geqslant1$; $\hat\nu_{j,k}$ denotes its estimate. In Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}: In part (i), the gap law $\mu_{1,k\mid X}$ is continuous at the two boundaries $\nu_{j,k}(X)$ and $\nu_{j+1,k}(X)$ used in each band, almost surely. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In the continuous-outcome part, the relevant treated conditional laws have uniformly bounded densities in fixed neighborhoods of $\nu_{j,k}(X)$ for $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, and no atom at the fixed boundary $\nu_{0,k}=y_{\min}$; only the boundaries with $j\geqslant1$ are estimated. — Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} fixes the indices and lower boundary. Flat.tex lines 1796--1810 distinguish true and fitted boundaries and state that the structural threshold is never estimated; Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}(i) specifies the local density and no-atom conditions.
- reading of the symbol `P_{1,k}`: For $k=1,\ldots,K$, $P_{1,k}(x)=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}[YW\mid Z\in S_k,X=x]$ is the population conditional treated-success regression in the binary-outcome setting, while $P_{1,0}(x)\equiv0$ is a structural boundary convention rather than a conditional regression on $S_0$. — Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}(ii) defines the regression and its zero boundary; Section~\ref{app:scores_discrete} states that the boundary residual does not involve a conditional regression on $S_0$.
- reading of the symbol `h_{j,k}^{-}`: $h_{j,k}^{-}(x)$ is the binary-outcome population subinterval contribution for $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=1,\ldots,l_k$, with $q_{0,k}(x)=p_k(x)$, and equals $J_{j-1,k}(x)$. — Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}\textup{(ii)} defines $h_{j,k}^{-}$ and its index ranges; the policy-level conventions define $q_{0,k}=p_k$. Section~\ref{app:scores_discrete} differentiates this same function.
- reading of the symbol `n`: $n$ is the total observed sample size indexing the estimation sequences and their asymptotics. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $n$ ranges over sample sizes permitting the stipulated equal-half split, so $n$ is even. — Line 1771 specifies a dataset of size $n$ and two sets each of size $n/2$; lines 908 and 1882 express the three-way split sizes relative to the same total $n$.
- reading of the symbol `I_0`: $I_0$ denotes the latent interval $(p_0,p_1]=(0,p_1]$ in the latent-interval decomposition and a sample index set contained in $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ in sample splitting. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $I_0$ is the sample index set in the three-way partition $I_0,I_1,I_2$; $I_0\cup I_1$ supplies the training samples on which the target-evaluation expectations are conditioned, and $I_2$ supplies evaluation observations, with $\|I_j\|/n\to\rho_j\in(0,1)$ for $j=0,1,2$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $I_0$ trains $\hat p$ and the derived policy and endpoint fits; conditional on this training sample, $I_1$ trains the outcome regression and treated boundary-law learner. — Line 474 labels the latent interval. The paragraph preceding equation~\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument} introduces three-way sample splitting and assigns training and evaluation roles; Algorithm~\ref{alg:continuous_instrument} repeats these roles. Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate} specifies the limiting split proportions. Line 2575 returns to the latent-interval meaning in an example.
- reading of the symbol `I_1`: $I_1$ denotes $(p_1,p_2]$ in the latent-resistance decomposition and a subset of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ in the estimation procedures. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $I_1$ is the auxiliary sample index set in the random disjoint partition into $I_1$ and $I_2$, each of size $n/2$; the propensity and remaining score nuisances are fitted on $I_1$, with level recovery using the covariates in $I_2$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $I_1$ is the nuisance sample index set in the three-way partition into $I_0,I_1,I_2$, with $\|I_1\|/n\to\rho_1\in(0,1)$; $I_0\cup I_1$ supplies the training samples and $I_2$ supplies target evaluation. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: Conditional on the propensity fit from $I_0$, $I_1$ is used to fit $\hat g_1$ and the treated boundary law. — Flat.tex line 476 labels the latent interval; line 834 changes to the auxiliary sample. Line 1771 specifies the equal two-way split, while lines 908 and 1882 specify the three-way split and the role of $I_1$.
- reading of the symbol `I_2`: $I_2$ denotes the latent interval $(p_2,p_3]$ in the interval decomposition and the main evaluation sample index set in estimation. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $I_2\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}$ is the main estimation index set, disjoint from $I_1$, with $I_1\cup I_2=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $\|I_2\|=n/2$; its covariates are also used to recover the finite level sets before evaluation scores are averaged over it. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $I_2\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}$ is the evaluation index set in the disjoint partition $I_0,I_1,I_2$, with $\|I_j\|/n\to\rho_j\in(0,1)$ for $j=0,1,2$; it evaluates the target using nuisances fitted on $I_0$ and $I_1$. — Flat.tex line 478 displays the latent interval. Line 834 introduces the estimation meaning; line 1771 specifies the equal-half partition, and lines 908 and 1882 specify the three-way partition. Lines 2195--2196 distinguish feasible evaluation from the oracle construction.
- reading of the symbol `\eta`: $\eta$ collects the nuisance functions entering the applicable bound and estimation formula, with its components determined by the instrument and outcome regime and $\hat\eta$ denoting their fitted counterparts. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In part~\textup{(i)}, $\eta(x)=\{p(z,x),p_k(x),\pi_k(x),\tau_{j,k}(x),a_{j,k}(z,x),\gamma_{\text{full},k}(x),\gamma_{j,k}(x),\gamma_K(x),\nu_{j,k}(x),M_{j,k}^{\pm}(x),J_{j,k}^{\pm}(x),J_{\text{full},k}^{\pm}(x)\}_{j,k}$; $\pi_k$ and minus-side objects are included only for $k\geqslant1$, and estimated quantiles have $j\geqslant1$, with structural boundary values fixed. In part~\textup{(ii)}, $\eta(x)=\{p(z,x),p_k(x),\pi_k(x),\tau_{j,k}(x),a_{j,k}(z,x),\gamma_{\text{full},k}(x),\gamma_{j,k}(x),\gamma_K(x),P_{1,k}(x)\}_{j,k}$, with $p_0=P_{1,0}=0$ and no fitted $\pi_0$; within-set share directions satisfy $\sum_{z\in T_{j,k}}\delta a_{j,k}(z,x)=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: The unaccented $\eta$ in $\sigma^2=\mathbb E[(\psi(O;\eta)-\underline\theta_{\omega,1})^2]$ denotes $\eta_0$, the true nuisance vector. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: The nuisance functions are those entering the connected-instrument plug-in representation: $p$, the policy propensity and support endpoints derived from it, $g_1$, and the treated boundary law or its quantile function. — The opening of Section~\ref{sec:est_inf} defines $\eta$ for estimation generally; (\ref{eq:continuous_simplified_target}) and (\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument}) specify the connected-instrument nuisance functions.
- reading of the symbol `\hat\eta`: $\hat\eta$ is the vector of fitted nuisance functions entering the applicable bound estimator, with its components determined by the instrument and outcome regime. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: The first stage is fitted on $I_1$ and the remaining nuisances are fitted on $I_1$ given the recovered level sets, subject to the stated clipping conventions. Feasible recovery uses $\{X_i:i\in I_2\}$; in the oracle procedure the population level sets are used and $\hat\eta$ is fixed conditional on $I_1$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: The fitted nuisances enter (\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument}), including estimates of $p$, the derived policy and support endpoints, $g_1$, and the treated boundary law or its quantile function; they are trained on $I_0\cup I_1$ and held fixed conditional on these training samples for evaluation on $I_2$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: The concrete procedure fits $p$ on $I_0$ and, conditional on that first stage, fits $g_1$ and the treated boundary law on $I_1$, with predictions clipped to their stated supports. — The opening of Section~\ref{sec:est_inf} introduces the nuisance vector across estimation regimes. The paragraph preceding (\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument}) specifies three-way training for connected instruments, and the following paragraph permits general nuisance estimators trained on $I_0\cup I_1$.
- reading of the symbol `\eta_0`: $\eta_0$ is the vector of population nuisance values for the applicable score, including its known structural-boundary conventions, distinct from the fitted vector $\hat\eta$. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: In part~\textup{(i)}, $\eta_0$ supplies the true nuisances for the continuous-outcome score, including boundary quantiles and conditional indicator and treated-outcome regressions; in part~\textup{(ii)}, it supplies the binary-outcome nuisances $p_k$, $P_{1,k}$, $\pi_k$, $\tau_{j,k}$, $a_{j,k}$, $\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}$, $\gamma_{j,k}$, and $\gamma_K$. — Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete} defines the true nuisance vector. Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality} specifies the two nuisance regimes, and its two proofs repeat the same definition while distinguishing $\hat\eta$.
- reading of the symbol `\delta`: The notation $\delta$ denotes a nuisance perturbation direction, whereas the indexed notation $\delta_n$ denotes a fixed boundary-localization window sequence. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\delta$ lies in the same function space as $\eta$ and satisfies that $\eta_0+t\delta$ is admissible for every $t$ near zero; fixed structural components have zero perturbation, and within-set share components satisfy $\sum_{z\in T_{j,k}}\delta a_z(X)=0$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\delta_n\to0$ is the localization window in $\mathcal I_{\delta,1}=\{i\in I_1:W_i=1,\ \hat P_i\leqslant\hat{\underline p}(X_i)+\delta_n\}$, indexed by sample size $n$. — Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete} defines admissible directions, and its proof in Section~\ref{oa:orthogonality_continuous} specifies the function space, structural boundary and simplex restrictions. The construction in Section~\ref{app:procedure_continuous_instrument} defines the localized sample; Algorithm~\ref{alg:continuous_instrument} fixes the window, and Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate} requires $\delta_n\to0$.
- reading of the symbol `\mathcal T`: $\mathcal T$ consists of directions in the nuisance function space for which $\eta_0+t\delta$ remains admissible for every $t$ in a neighborhood of zero; these preserve fixed structural components and satisfy $\sum_{z\in T_{j,k}}\delta a_z(X)=0$ for each within-set share vector. — Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete} defines the two-sided admissibility requirement. The proof in Section~\ref{oa:orthogonality_continuous} excludes a $p_0$ direction and explicitly imposes the simplex tangent restriction; the binary-outcome proof imposes the same share restriction.
- reading of the symbol `\psi`: $\psi$ denotes the uncentered total population integrand associated with the estimator of $\underline\theta_{\omega,1}$, with its nuisance arguments supplied explicitly when needed. In Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality_discrete}, Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\psi(O;\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\bigl(\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{\mathrm{prod}}(O;\eta)+\sum_{j=1}^{l_k}\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}(O;\eta)\bigr)+\psi_{\Delta_K}(O;\eta)$, with the component formulas in Section~\ref{app:scores_continuous} for continuous outcomes and Section~\ref{app:scores_discrete} for binary outcomes; $\eta_0$ supplies the true nuisance values. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:continuous_primitive_rate}: $\psi(O)=-\int_{\min\{\underline p(X),q(Z,X)\}}^{\underline p(X)}Q_{1,\underline p\mid X}(v/\underline p(X))\,\mathrm dv+g_1(q^{\mathrm{tr}}(Z,X),X)-g_1(P,X)+y_{\min}(q(Z,X)-\overline p(X))_+$, with the first integral zero when $\underline p(X)=0$; this is the population plug-in integrand. — The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}(i) displays the total orthogonal score, (\ref{eq:binary_score}) specifies its binary counterpart, and the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate} defines $\psi=\psi_1+\psi_2+\psi_3$.
- reading of the symbol `\hat\psi`: $\hat\psi$ denotes the total fitted integrand averaged over $I_2$ to form $\hat{\underline\theta}_{\omega,1}$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}: $\hat\psi(O;\hat\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\bigl(\hat\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{\mathrm{prod}}(O;\hat\eta)+\sum_{j=1}^{l_k}\hat\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}(O;\hat\eta)\bigr)+\hat\psi_{\Delta_K}(O;\hat\eta)$, using the continuous-outcome or binary-outcome score formulas according to the outcome regime. In the oracle procedure used in the proof, this fitted function is fixed conditional on $I_1$; the feasible procedure uses recovered level sets. In Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate}: $\hat\psi=\hat\psi_1+\hat\psi_2+\hat\psi_3$, obtained by substituting fitted nuisances into the three terms of (\ref{eq:continuous_simplified_target}); this is the plug-in integrand, fixed conditional on $I_0,I_1$, rather than the finite-instrument orthogonal score. — Equations (\ref{eq:estimator_dml}) and (\ref{eq:estimator_continuous_instrument}), the variance formula in Theorem~\ref{thm:dml_normality}, and the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:continuous_rate} specify the two uses.
- reading of the symbol `J_{j,k}^{+}`: $J_{j,k}^{+}$ is the population treated conditional band-mean function at the true boundaries $\nu_{j,k}$ and $\nu_{j+1,k}$, indexed by $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=0,\ldots,l_k-1$, with conditioning on $Z\in S_{k+1}$, $W=1$, and $X=x$. — The conditional-expectation display at flat.tex lines 1818--1824 defines the function; Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}(i) gives the band indices, and the target aggregation at lines 2010--2014 uses $J_{j-1,k}$ for $j=1,\ldots,l_k$.
- reading of the symbol `J_{j,k}^{-}`: $J_{j,k}^{-}(x)$ is the population truncated first moment $\mathbb E[Y\indicator(\nu_{j,k}(x)<Y\leqslant\nu_{j+1,k}(x))\mid X=x,Z\in S_k,W=1]$ for $k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}$ and $j\in\{0,\ldots,l_k-1\}$, using population quantile boundaries, with its $k=0$ value defined as zero solely for notational convenience and every corresponding minus-side score term omitted. — The display at line 1821 defines the truncated moment; Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization}(i) supplies the band indices and population boundaries. Lines 1830 and 2021 state the structural-boundary omission and zero convention.
- reading of the symbol `J_{\text{full},k}^{+}`: $J_{\text{full},k}^{+}(x)$ is the population conditional mean $\mathbb E[Y\mid X=x,Z\in S_{k+1},W=1]$ for $k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$. — The treated-regression display at line 1826 defines the conditional mean; the interval indices in Proposition~\ref{prop:J_specialization} and the assembled estimator (\ref{eq:estimator_dml}) give $k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$.
- reading of the symbol `J_{\text{full},k}^{-}`: $J_{\text{full},k}^{-}(x)$ is the population conditional mean $\mathbb E[Y\mid X=x,Z\in S_k,W=1]$ for $k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}$, with its $k=0$ value defined as zero solely for notational convenience and every corresponding minus-side score term omitted. — The treated-regression displays at lines 1826--1830 define the conditional mean and omit the regression at $k=0$; the structural-boundary convention at line 2021 defines minus-side objects as zero solely for notational convenience, and line 2064 applies this convention to the full-interval score.
- reading of the symbol `M_{j,k}^{+}`: $M_{j,k}^{+}$ is the population conditional indicator-mean function at the true boundary $\nu_{j,k}$, indexed by $k=0,\ldots,K-1$ and $j=0,\ldots,l_k$, with conditioning on $Z\in S_{k+1}$, $W=1$, and $X=x$.
